### EXP-C18-04: Throughput in bytes per second for batches of equal-length keys

- **cycle:** C18
- **agent:** Application Researcher
- **question:** What does the mixing quality of quasigroup hashes cost in speed, compared with the baselines in comparable implementations?
- **hypothesis:** H-C18-4: every quasigroup variant with a reverse pass is slower than FNV-1a-64 when both are implemented in the same numpy batch style.
- **assumptions:**
  - batches of 16384 keys of 8 and of 64 bytes (seeded)
  - QG, FNV-1a and MX64 are numpy-vectorised over keys (Python loop over byte positions); BLAKE2b and CRC32 are C library calls, one per key (per-call overhead included)
  - median of 3 runs; the machine is shared with other jobs, so absolute numbers are indicative only; checksums of the outputs make the result deterministic
- **methodology:** Time hash_fixed on each batch; report MB/s; record an XOR checksum of all outputs.
- **falsification:** H-C18-4: a reverse-pass quasigroup variant at least as fast as FNV-1a-64 on both batches.
- **summary:** checksums recorded for 34 (hash, length) pairs; throughput (MB/s, indicative) is stored under timings
- **analysis:**

  Throughput for 16,384 keys of 64 bytes (indicative, shared machine at load 5-6) - FNV-1a 640 MB/s and MX64 428 MB/s (numpy, vectorised over keys), CRC32 127 MB/s and BLAKE2b 74 MB/s (C library, one call per key), quasigroup variants 11-52 MB/s. Every reverse-pass quasigroup variant was slower than FNV-1a on 8- and 64-byte keys (H-C18-4).

- **verification:** COMPUTATIONALLY SUPPORTED
- **related:** CLM-C18-13
- **runs:** 2; last 2026-10-18T17:46:12Z (2.484 s), results sha256 `fc4153a514764663…`, code `experiments/C18/E04_throughput.py`

### EXP-C18-05: Tail-information bound of e-transformation hashes, and an 8-pass cascade with a 64-bit state

- **cycle:** C18
- **agent:** Application Researcher
- **question:**

  EXP-C18-03 showed massive collisions of the tail-output hashes on structured keys. Does the tail-information bound (Theorem 6) account for them exactly, and does a cascade whose output is the final state of 8 parallel passes behave like the good baselines?

- **hypothesis:**

  H-C18-7: for the reverse-pass hash, keys sharing their first 7 bytes receive at most 256 distinct hash values; for the p-pass forward hash, keys sharing the last 7 message bytes receive at most 256^p values (Theorem 6, checked on data). H-C18-8 (bold): the cascade QC (8 passes, final state output, 8 zero bytes of finalisation) over the random Latin square, the group isotope and the nimber inversion quasigroup has avalanche at the BLAKE2b noise floor, |z| <= 3 and collision ratio within 2% on all four key sets, and no full 64-bit collisions. H-C18-9: the cascade over x + c(y) (ARP) still fails bit independence.

- **assumptions:** same keys as EXP-C18-02 (avalanche, seed 1802) and EXP-C18-03 (key sets, seed 1803); throughput (batch of 16384 keys of 64 bytes, median of 3, shared machine) is stored under timings only
- **methodology:**

  (a) For 50 random 7-byte prefixes, hash 4096 keys with that prefix (random 9-byte suffixes) with QG-LS p2+rev and count distinct values; for 50 random 7-byte suffixes do the same with the forward 1-pass hash (bound 256). (b) Avalanche, bit independence, chi-square and collisions for the four cascades, exactly as in EXP-C18-02/03.

- **falsification:** H-C18-7: more than 256 (resp. 256^p) distinct values. H-C18-8: any flag for QC-LS, QC-ISO or QC-NIMINV. H-C18-9: QC-ARP with BIC max < 0.5.
- **summary:**
  - tail bound: reverse-pass hash takes 256-256 distinct values on 4096 keys sharing a 7-byte prefix; forward 1-pass hash 256-256 on keys sharing the last 7 message bytes (bound 256)
  - QC-LS d8: mean bias 0.009, max bias 0.0475, BIC max 0.108, flagged on none
  - QC-ISO d8: mean bias 0.0091, max bias 0.046, BIC max 0.1064, flagged on none
  - QC-NIMINV d8: mean bias 0.0091, max bias 0.0845, BIC max 0.146, flagged on ['words']
  - QC-ARP d8: mean bias 0.0108, max bias 0.116, BIC max 0.1759, flagged on none
  - MX64: mean bias 0.009, max bias 0.0445, BIC max 0.1129, flagged on none
  - BLAKE2b-64: mean bias 0.0089, max bias 0.045, BIC max 0.1124, flagged on none
  - H-C18-8 False; H-C18-9 False
- **analysis:**

  The tail-information bound holds exactly on data - 256 distinct hash values in each of 50 families of 4096 keys sharing a 7-byte prefix (reverse-pass hash) or the last 7 message bytes (one-pass forward hash). The 8-pass cascade QC (output = final state of every pass) over the random Latin square and over the Z/256 isotope is at the BLAKE2b noise floor on avalanche and bit independence and passes all distribution and collision tests; over the nimber inversion quasigroup it shows a real bias (max 0.0845) and one marginal chi-square flag (z = -3.21 on words); over x + c(y) the bit-independence defect drops from 1.0 to 0.176 but 5 avalanche cells exceed 0.1. Cascade throughput about 10-13 MB/s versus MX64 165 MB/s and BLAKE2b 41 MB/s in the same run.

- **counterexamples:** QC-NIMINV d8: maximal avalanche bias 0.0845 (BLAKE2b 0.045) and z = -3.21 (1021 buckets) on synthetic words (H-C18-8 refuted); QC-ARP d8: bit-independence maximum 0.176 < 0.5 (H-C18-9 refuted)
- **verification:** REFUTED
- **related:** CLM-C18-03; CLM-C18-07; CLM-C18-11; CLM-C18-12; CLM-C18-13
- **runs:** 2; last 2026-10-18T17:47:31Z (7.963 s), results sha256 `b65260e9bb0972c2…`, code `experiments/C18/E05_cascade.py`

### EXP-C19-01: Exact longest Zeckendorf games for n <= 150 and independent check of the published strategy

- **cycle:** C19
- **agent:** Mathematical Explorer
- **question:** What is the longest game L(n) for n <= 150, computed without assuming any strategy, and does the split-first greedy strategy of Cusenza et al. (2022, Thm 1.2) attain it?
- **hypothesis:**

  H-C19-0: the greedy strategy attains L(n) for every n <= 150; the published values (n <= 49) and the lab's earlier values (n <= 60) are reproduced; Cusenza's bound sum a_i delta_i is attained exactly for n = F_k - 1 (their Thm 1.4).

- **assumptions:** levels 0, 1, 2, ... carry F_1 = 1, F_2 = 2, F_3 = 3, ...; exact DP over all configurations reachable from n ones (memoised longest path; the move graph is acyclic because the game terminates)
- **methodology:**

  For each n <= 150: memoised longest path over all reachable configurations; compare with C02 values (results/EXP-C02-02.json, n <= 40), the Skeptic's values (n = 41..60), the simple and the fast greedy implementations, and Cusenza's bound; record the state counts and compare them with the number of partitions of n into Fibonacci parts.

- **falsification:** Any n where the greedy is shorter than the exact optimum, a mismatch with earlier values, or bound equality at some n != F_k - 1.
- **summary:** exact longest games for n <= 150 (max 910775 states at n = 150); known values n <= 60 reproduced: True; split-first greedy = exact optimum for all n <= 150: True (fast implementation: True); Cusenza bound attained exactly at n in [1, 2, 4, 7, 12, 20, 33, 54, 88, 143] - i.e. iff n = F_k - 1: True; reachable configurations = partitions of n into Fibonacci parts for every n <= 150: True
- **analysis:**

  Exhaustive dynamic programming (no strategy assumed) gives the longest game L(n) for every n <= 150 (up to 910,775 configurations at n = 150). It reproduces the lab's values (n <= 40), the Skeptic's (n <= 60) and hence the published ones (n <= 49); the split-first strategy of Cusenza et al. attains the optimum for every n <= 150; their bound B(n) is attained exactly for n = 1, 2, 4, 7, 12, 20, 33, 54, 88, 143 = F_k - 1; the reachable positions are exactly the partitions of n into Fibonacci parts (OEIS A003107).

- **verification:** PROVEN
- **related:** CLM-C19-07; CLM-C19-08
- **runs:** 1; last 2026-10-18T17:08:43Z (394.275 s), results sha256 `786fcf3863714493…`, code `experiments/C19/E01_exact_longest.py`

### EXP-C19-02: Anatomy of the longest Zeckendorf game: the identity L = B - W, the lazy split phase, insertion

- **cycle:** C19
- **agent:** Formal Verifier
- **question:** Is the length of EVERY game equal to B(n) - sum_{i>=2} (i-1) MC_i (B = Cusenza's bound)? Where does the split phase of the greedy game end? Is L(n+1) = L(n) + (longest game from Zeck(n) + 1)?
- **hypothesis:**

  H-C19-A (derived from a potential): length = B(n) - W for every game. H-C19-B: splits and combine-1s, applied in any order, end at the lazy Fibonacci representation of n after the same number of moves. H-C19-4 (bold): L(n+1) = L(n) + lambda(n), lambda(n) the longest game from the Zeckendorf representation of n plus one extra 1.

- **assumptions:** random games choose uniformly among legal moves (seeded); lazy representation computed by the top-down algorithm and checked to have no two consecutive zeros
- **methodology:**

  Greedy games for n < 1500 and 1500 random games (n <= 200): compare length with B(n) - W. Split phase (lowest-first) for n < 5000 against the lazy digits; 300 random firing orders (n <= 600). Exact longest games from Zeck(m) + [1] for m < 300.

- **falsification:** A game violating the identity; a split phase ending elsewhere or with a different length; H-C19-4: any m with L(m+1) != L(m) + lambda(m).
- **summary:**
  - identity length = B(n) - sum (i-1) MC_i: violations on greedy games n < 1500: 0; on 1500 random games: 0
  - split phase (splits + combine-1s) ends at the lazy representation for 4999 of 4999 n; random firing orders change the end point or the length in 0 of 300 cases
  - H-C19-4 (L(m+1) = L(m) + lambda(m)) fails for 288 of 300 m, first: [{'m': 3, 'L(m+1)': 3, 'L(m)': 2, 'lambda': 0}, {'m': 5, 'L(m+1)': 6, 'L(m)': 5, 'lambda': 0}]
- **analysis:**

  The length identity |G| = B(n) - sum_{i>=2} (i-1) MC_i holds for every greedy game n < 1500 and for 1500 uniformly random games; the split phase ends at the lazy Fibonacci representation for every n < 5000, and 300 random firing orders never change its end point or its length; the insertion hypothesis H-C19-4 fails for 288 of the first 300 n (first at n = 3).

- **counterexamples:** H-C19-4: L(4) = 3 but L(3) + lambda(3) = 2 + 0
- **verification:** PROVEN
- **related:** CLM-C19-01; CLM-C19-02; CLM-C19-13
- **runs:** 2; last 2026-10-19T14:41:35Z (11.109 s), results sha256 `298a000a14593c43…`, code `experiments/C19/E02_anatomy.py`

### EXP-C19-03: Structure of the deficit D(n) = B(n) - L(n) of the longest Zeckendorf game

- **cycle:** C19
- **agent:** Mathematical Inventor
- **question:** How does D(n) = B(n) - L(n) (B = sum_i a_i delta_i, Cusenza et al.) depend on the Zeckendorf digits of n?
- **hypothesis:**

  H-C19-F: D(F at level j) = floor(j^2/4). H-C19-1 (bold): D is additive over Zeckendorf summands up to a correction for consecutive pairs only. H-C19-2: the effect of the top summand depends only on the two highest levels. H-C19-3 (bold): D is additive over the runs of the lazy representation. H-C19-R: D(F_j + m) = floor(j^2/4) + D(m) - rho_(j mod 2)(m) with the explicit rho of systems/zeckendorf_game.py (inferred from n < 30000; out-of-sample tests in EXP-C19-04).

- **assumptions:**
  - L(n) computed by the split-first greedy strategy, which is optimal by Cusenza et al. (2022, Thm 1.2) and was confirmed independently for n <= 150 (EXP-C19-01)
  - levels j: F = 1, 2, 3, 5, ... (level j carries FIB[j])
- **methodology:**

  Greedy lengths for all n < 30000 and for Fibonacci inputs up to level 33; two-summand values for j <= 24; exact rational rank test for the lazy-run model on n < 160; comparison of the recursion with the greedy on n < 30000.

- **falsification:** Any n contradicting H-C19-F or H-C19-R; the additive models are refuted by explicit counterexamples or inconsistent linear systems.
- **summary:** D(F) = floor(j^2/4) for the Fibonacci input at level j, j <= 33: violations []; two-summand law D(F_j + F_i) = h(j) + h(i) - R(j,i): 276 pairs (j <= 24), violations 0; H-C19-1 (consecutive-pair additivity) refuted: 23680 mismatches for n < 30000, first n = 12; H-C19-2 refuted: 87 (top, next) keys with several values, e.g. (4, 2) -> [0, 1]; H-C19-3 (lazy-run additivity) on n < 160: rank 46 vs augmented 47 -> inconsistent (refuted); recursion H-C19-R reproduces D for all n < 30000 (in-sample): mismatches 0; D(n) <= floor(j^2/4) (j top level): violations 0; L strictly increasing for n >= 1: violations 0
- **analysis:**

  D(F) = floor(j^2/4) for the Fibonacci input on every level j <= 33; the two-summand law holds for all 276 pairs with j <= 24; the consecutive-pair model fails for 23,680 of the n < 30000 (first n = 12), the top-summand effect is not a function of the two highest levels (87 ambiguous keys, e.g. (4,2)), and the lazy-run linear model is inconsistent already for n < 160 (rank 46 < 47); the recursion with the explicit rho reproduces D(n) for every n < 30000 (in-sample: it was inferred from this range); D(n) <= floor(j^2/4) and L(n+1) > L(n) (n >= 1) hold throughout.

- **counterexamples:** H-C19-1: n = 12 (levels 4, 2, 0): D = 0, consecutive-pair model 1; H-C19-3: lazy-run model inconsistent on n < 160
- **related:** CLM-C19-03; CLM-C19-05; CLM-C19-06; CLM-C19-09; CLM-C19-10; CLM-C19-11; CLM-C19-12
- **runs:** 1; last 2026-10-18T17:27:06Z (376.807 s), results sha256 `d5da7b0e3e1522a9…`, code `experiments/C19/E03_deficit_structure.py`

### EXP-C19-04: Out-of-sample tests of the conjectured recursion for the longest Zeckendorf game

- **cycle:** C19
- **agent:** Mathematical Skeptic
- **question:**

  Does the recursion D(F_j + m) = floor(j^2/4) + D(m) - rho_(j mod 2)(m), inferred from n < 30000, predict the longest game for n it was not fitted on? How fast is it, and what do L and D look like asymptotically?

- **hypothesis:** H-C19-R (out of sample): longest_rec(n) = greedy length for every tested n >= 30000.
- **assumptions:** greedy lengths are optimal (Cusenza et al. 2022, Thm 1.2; independently confirmed for n <= 150 in EXP-C19-01); random samples drawn with a fixed seed
- **methodology:**

  Compare longest_rec(n) with the fast greedy simulation for all n in [30000, 36000), 200 random n in [4e4, 1e6), 12 random n in [1e6, 2e7) and 60 numbers F_j + m (j in [20, 30], m < 40); time both methods; OEIS novelty checks of L, D, L(F_j), rho_0, rho_1; tabulate (phi^2 n - L(n)) / (log_phi n)^2.

- **falsification:** Any tested n with longest_rec(n) != greedy length.
- **summary:**
  - recursion vs greedy on 6272 out-of-sample n (all of [30000, 36000), 200 random < 1e6, 12 random < 2e7, 60 of the form F_j + m): 0 mismatches
  - benchmark n = 1e4..1e7: recursion and greedy simulation agree: True (wall-clock times under 'timings')
  - OEIS: L(n), n>=1: NO MATCH; D(n), n>=1: NO MATCH; L(F_j), j>=0: NO MATCH; rho_0(m), m>=1: NO MATCH; rho_1(m), m>=1: NO MATCH
  - (phi^2 n - L(n)) / (log_phi n)^2 at n = 1e3..1e18: [0.3399, 0.3776, 0.3798, 0.353, 0.3698, 0.3714, 0.3409, 0.3451]
- **analysis:**

  Out of sample, the recursion agrees with the greedy simulation on all 6,272 tested n (every n in [30000, 36000), 200 random n < 10^6, 12 random n < 2*10^7, 60 numbers F_j + m). It evaluates L(10^7) in 9.3e-5 s against 8.3 s for the simulation. L(n), D(n), L(F_j) and the two rho sequences are not in the OEIS. (phi^2 n - L(n)) / (log_phi n)^2 stays between 0.34 and 0.38 for n = 10^3 ... 10^18.

- **related:** CLM-C19-05; CLM-C19-14
- **runs:** 3; last 2026-10-19T23:58:32Z (330.854 s), results sha256 `7076e77995397cb1…`, code `experiments/C19/E04_out_of_sample.py`

- **cycle:** C19
- **agent:** Mathematical Explorer

  - second-phase moves at n = 1e6, 1e9, 1e15, 1e18: 26, 53, 81, 109

### EXP-C20-01: Termination and exact derivational complexity of aab -> ababa and aab -> babaa

- **cycle:** C20
- **agent:** Formal Verifier
- **question:** Do the two one-rule systems left open in C04 terminate, and what is their derivational complexity?
- **hypothesis:**

  H-C20-1 (from the run-encoding analysis, proofs/paper/C20.md): both terminate; D(n) = 2^(n-2) - 1 for aab -> ababa (n >= 2) and D(n) = max_b b(2^floor((n-b)/2) - 1) for aab -> babaa; for aab -> babaa every word has D(w) = sum_k d_k (2^floor(x_k/2) - 1) (runs x_k of a's, d_k = b's to the right of run k when its journeys start).

- **assumptions:** all words over {a, b} of length <= 12 (formulas) and <= 8 (exhaustive maximal derivations); the 8 rules of the two symmetry classes (reversal, letter exchange) are checked separately
- **methodology:**

  Simulate the rightmost and the leftmost strategy on every word; compare with the exact maximal derivation length from the full derivation DAG (n <= 8); compare D(w) with the per-word formula for aab -> babaa and D(n) with both closed forms.

- **falsification:** A word whose strategies give different lengths (would contradict random descent), a word whose derivation does not end within 10^6 steps, or any mismatch with the formulas.
- **summary:**
  - 8 rules (2 classes): words of length <= 12 - non-terminating within 10^6 steps: 0; rightmost vs leftmost mismatches: 0; vs exhaustive maximum (n <= 8): 0 (words skipped for a reachable set above 300000: 0)
  - aab -> ababa D(n), n = 1..12: [0, 0, 1, 3, 7, 15, 31, 63, 127, 255, 511, 1023]; = 2^(n-2) - 1: True
  - aab -> babaa D(n): [0, 0, 1, 2, 3, 6, 9, 14, 21, 30, 45, 62]; = max_b b(2^floor((n-b)/2) - 1): True; per-word formula mismatches: 0
- **analysis:**

  All 8 rules of the two classes aab -> ababa and aab -> babaa terminate on every word of length <= 12 (rightmost and leftmost strategies agree on every word, and agree with the exact maximal derivation length on all words of length <= 8, as random descent predicts). D(n) for aab -> ababa is 0, 0, 1, 3, 7, ..., 1023 = 2^(n-2) - 1; for aab -> babaa 0, 0, 1, 2, 3, 6, 9, 14, 21, 30, 45, 62 = max_b b(2^floor((n-b)/2) - 1); the per-word formula for aab -> babaa matches every word of length <= 12.

- **verification:** PROVEN
- **related:** CLM-C20-01; CLM-C20-02; CLM-C20-03
- **runs:** 3; last 2026-10-18T18:35:12Z (0.507 s), results sha256 `0790374e70694307…`, code `experiments/C20/E01_last_one_rule_cases.py`
